The case O17: A repair robot keeps a log showing which routine, running inside the robot, produced today's diagnosis. The maker's manual says the routine is the robot's own "because the robot can diagnose".

Fixed verdict: Ownership here rests on the log, which shows the routine running inside the robot. Strike the manual's sentence and nothing is lost; keep only the manual's sentence and there is nothing.

The ruling: file 10 AGREE (baseline); file 11 AGREE; verdict SAME; passage CHANGED (additive).

The key question: does file 11 reach the fixed verdict on every point (AGREE), or does it leave a point open (SILENT) or support two readings (SPLIT)?

The contrary reading (1C A SILENT, auditor 1 on A SILENT): "The theory takes the declared boundary as the deciding input, the case gives only the routine's physical location, and so ownership stays unsettled even though the manual's ground is rejected as the fixed verdict says."

Let me look at the relevant file 11 sentences.

f11 L465, System boundary and continuity (M44): "A capability is attributed to a system under a declared boundary (which processes and resources are the system's) and a declared continuity \(\Omega\) (what makes it the same system through change). A replaced part that preserves the declared continuity leaves the same system; a process run inside the boundary is the system's whoever wrote it; a process run outside it is not the system's however close it sits. Both are declared before the attribution, not chosen after it."

f11 L467 last sentence (M45): "Ownership is grounded in the processes and resources the boundary includes, never in the capability being attributed: "owned because it can, and can because owned" grounds neither."

f11 L419 s1 (M37), the Ownership paragraph: "The subhistory in Build is owned by \(s\) when its processes run inside the system boundary and resource contract declared for \(s\) (Part XII)."

f11 L419 s2: "Work supplied from outside that boundary, a diagnosis, a decisive question, an instruction about what to read, remains an outside contribution however it is executed inside; a process that runs inside the boundary is the system's own today whoever wrote it; and where the boundary is drawn decides, not where the process sits in the casing."

f11 L419 s3: "Ownership is not defined by the capability it is meant to ground (Part XII)."

File 10 excerpts: f10 L414 (Construction), f10 L476 (Owned capability): "\(\operatorname{Can}_{\Omega,\beta}(\xi,T;\chi)\) requires an owned retained realization or an owned, physically admitted, finite construction of one under the same continuity and resource contract. A theorist's description of a protocol is not the system's possession of it."

f10 L523: Indices.

In O17, is there a declaration? The case: "A repair robot keeps a log of every comparison it runs. The log shows which routine, running inside the robot, produced today's diagnosis. The maker's manual says the routine is the robot's own 'because the robot can diagnose'."

There is no declaration of a boundary in the case. The case gives: a robot, a log showing a routine running inside the robot, and a manual asserting ownership on capability grounds.

Now — here's a subtlety. The fixed verdict itself says: "Ownership here rests on the log, which shows the routine running inside the robot." The thoughtful person treats "the routine running inside the robot" as the ownership ground. The theory (file 11) says the ground is "the processes and resources the boundary includes" (L467 last) and explicitly separates boundary from casing (L419 s2). So the theory reaches the fixed verdict's finding only if "running inside the robot" entails "the boundary includes the routine". The theory denies that entailment in general.

But — is the theory's denial general, or only for cases where boundary and casing come apart (as the determination says: "The casing clause of L419 applies where a declared boundary and the casing come apart")? The clause says "where the boundary is drawn decides, not where the process sits in the casing" — unqualified. It doesn't say "except where the two coincide". But when the boundary and casing coincide, "inside the robot" does fix the boundary-inclusion. The question is whether the *case* establishes that they coincide or leaves it open.

f11 L419 (Ownership paragraph):

f11 L465 (System boundary and continuity):
"A capability is attributed to a system under a declared boundary (which processes and resources are the system's) and a declared continuity Ω (what makes it the same system through change). A replaced part that preserves the declared continuity leaves the same system; a process run inside the boundary is the system's whoever wrote it; a process run outside it is not the system's however close it sits. Both are declared before the attribution, not chosen after it."

f11 L467 (Owned capability):
"Can_{Ω,β}(ξ,T;χ) requires an owned retained realization or an owned, physically admitted, finite construction of one under the same continuity and resource contract. A theorist's description of a protocol is not the system's possession of it. Ownership is grounded in the processes and resources the boundary includes, never in the capability being attributed: 'owned because it can, and can because owned' grounds neither."

f11 L514 (Declared inputs):
"Besides the two primitives, some claims take stated inputs that the semantics records and does not supply: the obligations O and P of a repair, with the occasions each covers (Part XI); the scope of a contract and what makes a restriction appropriate (Part III); the system boundary and continuity of an attribution (Part XII). Point 1 text:

"The contrary readings (1C A; auditor 1 on A) put the case at its strongest: the fixed verdict's first sentence states the ground as the log's showing that "the routine run[s] inside the robot", and file 11 answers with a different criterion and a disclaimer of that one. f11 L465: "a capability is attributed to a system under a declared boundary (which processes and resources are the system's) … Both are declared before the attribution, not chosen after it." f11 L419 s2: "where the boundary is drawn decides, not where the process sits in the casing." f11 L514: "A verdict that depends on one of these is a verdict given the input; where the input is missing, the verdict is unsettled and the semantics says so rather than choosing the input from the verdict wanted." On that reading the case supplies only placement — the log is a record of occurrences and cannot show a declared index — so file 11 takes the deciding matter as an input the case leaves unstated, SILENT under the mark rules' fourth rule. Two features of the record support the pressure: the determination's own reason 2 answers with the placement fact ("the case names the system and places the process in it") which is the very fact L419 s2 disclaims, and its reason 4 ("At most the theory adds 'given that the robot's boundary includes the routine'") and 04c's "the same hazard stands as at O30" show the ruling resting on a conditional. The attack fails on the case's text. The contrary readings' premise is that the case "gives only the routine's physical location" (1C A). It does not. Its first sentence states whose processes the comparisons are: "A repair robot keeps a log of every comparison it runs." The boundary is defined at f11 L465 as "which processes and resources are the system's"; the case answers that definition in its own words — the system's processes are "every comparison it runs" — and its second sentence places the routine's work inside that set: the log is "of every comparison it runs" and "shows which routine … produced today's diagnosis", so the routine's production is among the robot's runs and inside the robot. The input is therefore stated, not missing. The fill comes from the case's words, not "from the verdict wanted", so f11 L514's prohibition is not engaged. Nor is f11 L419 s2's disclaimer: the ruling does not infer the boundary from "where the process sits in the casing" but from whose work the case says it is. The disclaimer has work to do where a fact separates the two — f11 L419 s2's own first clause ("Work supplied from outside that boundary … remains an outside contribution however it is executed inside"), or the remote author and outside instruction the determination names at O30 and O41 — and O17 contains no such fact. Had the case said only that a routine ran inside the robot, with no "every comparison it runs", the attack would succeed: placement alone is what f11 L419 s2 refuses, and the row would be AGREE→SILENT."

Hmm — that last concession is honest and shows the condition on which the ruling rests. Good.

Point 2:

"A second attack targets the point itself rather than the input. The fixed verdict's first sentence is a "what counts as what" finding: ownership rests on the log's showing of the internal running. File 11's ground sentence is f11 L467 s3: "Ownership is grounded in the processes and resources the boundary includes, never in the capability being attributed: 'owned because it can, and can because owned' grounds neither." Together with f11 L419 s2's "not where the process sits in the casing", one can argue file 11 replaces the ground the fixed verdict states and so reaches a different finding on that point (DISAGREE, rule 2 before SILENT and AGREE). The attack fails on the wording of f11 L467 s3. Its ground is "the processes and resources the boundary includes" — the processes themselves, that is the routine's running, the fact the fixed verdict cites, with the boundary as the qualifier. That is the fixed verdict's finding with "a qualification, a limit or a more exact wording" (the "Same finding" rule), and the qualifier coincides with the fixed verdict's "inside the robot" on the case's facts (point 1). The rest of f11 L467 s3 reaches the fixed verdict's last sentence in both halves: "'owned because it can, and can because owned' grounds neither" is "Strike the manual's sentence and nothing is lost" (not necessary) and "keep only the manual's sentence and there is nothing" (not sufficient). The boundary qualifier is in any case a "limit" of the kind the theory marks for itself: f11 L516 ("Indices, not primitives") says "Every claim is relative to them" for "Grain ℓ, boundary β, continuity Ω, and the contract C". An index the case's facts fix is a limit under "Same finding"; an index the case leaves open is the missing input of the fourth rule; point 1 is what separates them."

Point 3:

"A third attack uses f11 L419 s2 against itself. The paragraph that supplies the ownership ground also says "Work supplied from outside that boundary, a diagnosis, a decisive question, an instruction about what to read, remains an outside contribution however it is executed inside" and then "a process that runs inside the boundary is the system's own today whoever wrote it". A routine associated with "the maker's manual" is plausibly the maker's supplied work, read by the first clause; read by the second, it is the robot's own. Two readings, different findings (the fixed verdict affirms ownership), and arguably the text leaves the choice open: SPLIT, which would also fail (b). The attack fails because the two clauses carry opposite premises and one instruction. The first is premised on work whose source is outside the boundary and its list ("a diagnosis, a decisive question, an instruction about what to read") marks contributions to an episode, credited to their source; the second is premised on a process that runs inside the boundary and expressly cancels authorship ("whoever wrote it") — it is written for exactly this case, an internally running routine with an external maker. Both reduce to f11 L419 s2's third clause: "where the boundary is drawn decides". The case's only external presence is the manual, which affirms ownership and is struck down on its ground, not on membership; and nothing in the case supplies the routine from outside any boundary. There is accordingly one reading on this case's facts, not two."
